It’s more—well, it’s not something I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nDoctor: Is it the act of driving on the bridges that scares you, or would you feel the same fear if you were a passenger on the same bridges?\n\nPatient: It’s worse if I’m the one driving, I think, because then I’m responsible for keeping the car steady and not hesitating. But honestly, I’ve started arranging things so someone else drives me over those bridges if I have to go.\n\nI’ve turned down gallery openings, birdwatching trips—there was one I really wanted to go to—and even some visits with my daughter unless I can get someone to take me. Which is embarrassing, because I used to be the one organizing everything, maps and times and all of it. Now I’m asking, “Are you driving?” before I even decide whether I’m going.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge, rather than driving?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really walked on one of those bridges, not the big ones going toward Miami. At my age I’m not strolling over bridges much—though I used to walk everywhere when I was younger, even in heels, which seems impossible now.\n\nBut what finally pushed me to come talk to you is what happens in the car. The moment my car gets onto one of those long bridges, my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp down on the wheel. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off it, right then, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less intense if you were driving on a bridge that is not over water?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less… well, I’m not completely sure, because the ones I’m avoiding are the ones over the water. It’s that stretch of open water underneath that gets to me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and everybody crosses them all day long like it’s nothing. My daughter will say, “Mother, it’s perfectly safe,” and I know that in my head. But when I’m the one facing driving over all that water, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A regular overpass or something doesn’t seem to have that same feeling for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

But lately, when my car gets to one of those long bridges heading toward Miami, I just—my chest tightens right away, and my hands grip the wheel so hard. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off the bridge, even though of course there’s nowhere to get off. That’s what finally made me think I ought to come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that cause you this fear, or are there other driving situations that make you feel this way?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly those bridges, yes—especially the long ones over water. I mean, I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years, and everyone drives over them all day long without making a fuss. In my head I know it shouldn’t be such a terrible thing, but it feels enormous in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than when you're driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them the same, no. Yes, I think it would be less if it were over land. It’s really the open water underneath that seems to do it to me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s the driving, really… though I’ve been driving a long time, you know, and my husband used to say I was the steadier one. But lately, when my car gets to one of those long bridges heading toward Miami, I just—my chest tightens right away, and my hands grip the wheel so hard. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off the bridge, even though of course there’s nowhere to get off. That’s what finally made me think I ought to come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that cause you this fear, or are there other driving situations that make you feel this way?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly those bridges, yes—especially the long ones over water. I mean, I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years, and everyone drives over them all day long without making a fuss. My neighbor’s daughter goes over into Miami constantly, like it’s nothing—she’s always rushing somewhere, though that’s young people, isn’t it?\n\nBut when I think about being out there over all that open water, it just feels… much bigger than any real danger. In my head I know it shouldn’t be such a terrible thing, but it feels enormous in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than when you're driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them the same, no. It’s really the bridges that stand out to me. I mean, I can get nervous about plenty of things—at my age there’s always some errand or appointment to think about, and parking lots have become such a circus—but that sharp, overwhelming feeling happens when I’m on those bridges, especially going toward Miami.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful in other high places, like tall buildings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, tall buildings aren’t something I deal with much anymore. Years ago I worked in an office that had quite a view, and I remember people crowding by the windows when there were fireworks, which always seemed silly to me because you could barely hear them through the glass.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed that same sort of fear just being up high in a building. It’s more—well, it’s not something I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nDoctor: Is it the act of driving on the bridges that scares you, or would you feel the same fear if you were a passenger on the same bridges?\n\nPatient: It’s worse if I’m the one driving, I think, because then I’m responsible for keeping the car steady and not hesitating. But honestly, I’ve started arranging things so someone else drives me over those bridges if I have to go.\n\nI’ve turned down gallery openings, birdwatching trips—there was one I really wanted to go to—and even some visits with my daughter unless I can get someone to take me. Which is embarrassing, because I used to be the one organizing everything, maps and times and all of it. Now I’m asking, “Are you driving?” before I even decide whether I’m going.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge, rather than driving?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really walked on one of those bridges, not the big ones going toward Miami.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, really. A very deep canyon sounds unpleasant, I suppose, but I keep coming back to the water. That’s the part that gets into my head.

I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years and people cross them every day—my daughter reminds me of that, as if I don’t know—but when I think about driving over all that open water, the fear feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A dry canyon might make me uneasy, but it doesn’t have quite the same feeling in my mind.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F40.228\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s the driving, really… though I’ve been driving a long time, you know, and my husband used to say I was the steadier one. But lately, when my car gets to one of those long bridges heading toward Miami, I just—my chest tightens right away, and my hands grip the wheel so hard. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off the bridge, even though of course there’s nowhere to get off. That’s what finally made me think I ought to come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that cause you this fear, or are there other driving situations that make you feel this way?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly those bridges, yes—especially the long ones over water. I mean, I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years, and everyone drives over them all day long without making a fuss. My neighbor’s daughter goes over into Miami constantly, like it’s nothing—she’s always rushing somewhere, though that’s young people, isn’t it?\n\nBut when I think about being out there over all that open water, it just feels… much bigger than any real danger. In my head I know it shouldn’t be such a terrible thing, but it feels enormous in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than when you're driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them the same, no. It’s really the bridges that stand out to me. I mean, I can get nervous about plenty of things—at my age there’s always some errand or appointment to think about, and parking lots have become such a circus—but that sharp, overwhelming feeling happens when I’m on those bridges, especially going toward Miami.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful in other high places, like tall buildings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, tall buildings aren’t something I deal with much anymore. Years ago I worked in an office that had quite a view, and I remember people crowding by the windows when there were fireworks, which always seemed silly to me because you could barely hear them through the glass.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed that same sort of fear just being up high in a building. It’s more—well, it’s not something I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nDoctor: Is it the act of driving on the bridges that scares you, or would you feel the same fear if you were a passenger on the same bridges?\n\nPatient: It’s worse if I’m the one driving, I think, because then I’m responsible for keeping the car steady and not hesitating. But honestly, I’ve started arranging things so someone else drives me over those bridges if I have to go.\n\nI’ve turned down gallery openings, birdwatching trips—there was one I really wanted to go to—and even some visits with my daughter unless I can get someone to take me. Which is embarrassing, because I used to be the one organizing everything, maps and times and all of it. Now I’m asking, “Are you driving?” before I even decide whether I’m going.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge, rather than driving?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really walked on one of those bridges, not the big ones going toward Miami. At my age I’m not strolling over bridges much—though I used to walk everywhere when I was younger, even in heels, which seems impossible now.\n\nBut what finally pushed me to come talk to you is what happens in the car. The moment my car gets onto one of those long bridges, my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp down on the wheel. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off it, right then, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less intense if you were driving on a bridge that is not over water?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less… well, I’m not completely sure, because the ones I’m avoiding are the ones over the water. It’s that stretch of open water underneath that gets to me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and everybody crosses them all day long like it’s nothing. My daughter will say, “Mother, it’s perfectly safe,” and I know that in my head. But when I’m the one facing driving over all that water, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A regular overpass or something doesn’t seem to have that same feeling for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious or fearful if you were in a tunnel?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I can’t say I’ve paid much attention to tunnels one way or the other. We don’t have many that I use regularly, at least not in my usual errands.\n\nI did go through some years ago on a trip—my husband used to like planning routes, and he’d always say the scenic way was better, though it usually added an hour—but I don’t remember feeling anything like what happens with those bridges. So… I’m not sure I can give you a very good answer about tunnels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would be less intense if the bridge was over land?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be less if it were over land. It’s really the open water underneath that seems to do it to me.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges have been there forever, and people drive across them all day without giving it a second thought—my daughter does, everyone does. But when I imagine myself driving over all that water, the fear just feels… much larger than the actual danger. Over land, I don’t think it would grab me the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a bridge over a very deep but dry canyon?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. A very deep canyon sounds unpleasant, I suppose, but I keep coming back to the water. That’s the part that gets into my head.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years and people cross them every day—my daughter reminds me of that, as if I don’t know—but when I think about driving over all that open water, the fear feels so much bigger than the actual danger. It’s not quite as bad if someone else is driving, I think, but I still don’t like it. I’ve actually started turning down things—gallery openings, birdwatching trips, even sometimes visits with my daughter—unless I know someone else can take me over the bridges.

Which is embarrassing, honestly, because I used to be the one who planned everything. My husband would forget half the details and I’d have the maps, the times, the parking figured out… and now I’m fussing about whether there’s a bridge on the way. So no, I wouldn’t say it feels the same as when I’m driving, but it’s still enough that I arrange my life around it more than I’d like.

I mean, I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years, and everyone drives over them all day long without making a fuss. My neighbor’s daughter goes over into Miami constantly, like it’s nothing—she’s always rushing somewhere, though that’s young people, isn’t it?\n\nBut when I think about being out there over all that open water, it just feels… much bigger than any real danger. In my head I know it shouldn’t be such a terrible thing, but it feels enormous in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than when you're driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them the same, no. It’s really the bridges that stand out to me. I mean, I can get nervous about plenty of things—at my age there’s always some errand or appointment to think about, and parking lots have become such a circus—but that sharp, overwhelming feeling happens when I’m on those bridges, especially going toward Miami.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful in other high places, like tall buildings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, tall buildings aren’t something I deal with much anymore. Years ago I worked in an office that had quite a view, and I remember people crowding by the windows when there were fireworks, which always seemed silly to me because you could barely hear them through the glass.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed that same sort of fear just being up high in a building. It’s more—well, it’s not something I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nDoctor: Is it the act of driving on the bridges that scares you, or would you feel the same fear if you were a passenger on the same bridges?\n\nPatient: It’s worse if I’m the one driving, I think, because then I’m responsible for keeping the car steady and not hesitating. But honestly, I’ve started arranging things so someone else drives me over those bridges if I have to go.\n\nI’ve turned down gallery openings, birdwatching trips—there was one I really wanted to go to—and even some visits with my daughter unless I can get someone to take me. Which is embarrassing, because I used to be the one organizing everything, maps and times and all of it. Now I’m asking, “Are you driving?” before I even decide whether I’m going.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge, rather than driving?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really walked on one of those bridges, not the big ones going toward Miami. At my age I’m not strolling over bridges much—though I used to walk everywhere when I was younger, even in heels, which seems impossible now.\n\nBut what finally pushed me to come talk to you is what happens in the car. The moment my car gets onto one of those long bridges, my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp down on the wheel. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off it, right then, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less intense if you were driving on a bridge that is not over water?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less… well, I’m not completely sure, because the ones I’m avoiding are the ones over the water. It’s that stretch of open water underneath that gets to me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and everybody crosses them all day long like it’s nothing. My daughter will say, “Mother, it’s perfectly safe,” and I know that in my head. But when I’m the one facing driving over all that water, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A regular overpass or something doesn’t seem to have that same feeling for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious or fearful if you were in a tunnel?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I can’t say I’ve paid much attention to tunnels one way or the other. We don’t have many that I use regularly, at least not in my usual errands.\n\nI did go through some years ago on a trip—my husband used to like planning routes, and he’d always say the scenic way was better, though it usually added an hour—but I don’t remember feeling anything like what happens with those bridges. So… I’m not sure I can give you a very good answer about tunnels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would be less intense if the bridge was over land?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be less if it were over land. It’s really the open water underneath that seems to do it to me.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges have been there forever, and people drive across them all day without giving it a second thought—my daughter does, everyone does. But when I imagine myself driving over all that water, the fear just feels… much larger than the actual danger. Over land, I don’t think it would grab me the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a bridge over a very deep but dry canyon?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. A very deep canyon sounds unpleasant, I suppose, but I keep coming back to the water. That’s the part that gets into my head.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years and people cross them every day—my daughter reminds me of that, as if I don’t know—but when I think about driving over all that open water, the fear feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A dry canyon might make me uneasy, but it doesn’t have quite the same feeling in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if someone else was driving you over a bridge with a similar height and water underneath, but you were not driving?\n\nPatient: It’s not quite as bad if someone else is driving, I think, but I still don’t like it. I’ve actually started turning down things—gallery openings, birdwatching trips, even sometimes visits with my daughter—unless I know someone else can take me over the bridges.\n\nWhich is embarrassing, honestly, because I used to be the one who planned everything. My husband would forget half the details and I’d have the maps, the times, the parking figured out… and now I’m fussing about whether there’s a bridge on the way. So no, I wouldn’t say it feels the same as when I’m driving, but it’s still enough that I arrange my life around it more than I’d like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

A very high bridge over land… I suppose I wouldn’t love it, no, but it’s the water that really makes my mind run away with itself.

I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without anything happening. My daughter says that to me too, very sensibly. But when I picture myself driving over all that open water, that’s when the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger. Over land, even if it was high, I don’t think it would hit me in quite the same way.

I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off it, right then, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less intense if you were driving on a bridge that is not over water?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less… well, I’m not completely sure, because the ones I’m avoiding are the ones over the water. It’s that stretch of open water underneath that gets to me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and everybody crosses them all day long like it’s nothing. My daughter will say, “Mother, it’s perfectly safe,” and I know that in my head. But when I’m the one facing driving over all that water, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A regular overpass or something doesn’t seem to have that same feeling for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious or fearful if you were in a tunnel?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I can’t say I’ve paid much attention to tunnels one way or the other. We don’t have many that I use regularly, at least not in my usual errands.\n\nI did go through some years ago on a trip—my husband used to like planning routes, and he’d always say the scenic way was better, though it usually added an hour—but I don’t remember feeling anything like what happens with those bridges. So… I’m not sure I can give you a very good answer about tunnels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would be less intense if the bridge was over land?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be less if it were over land. It’s really the open water underneath that seems to do it to me.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges have been there forever, and people drive across them all day without giving it a second thought—my daughter does, everyone does. But when I imagine myself driving over all that water, the fear just feels… much larger than the actual danger. Over land, I don’t think it would grab me the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a bridge over a very deep but dry canyon?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. A very deep canyon sounds unpleasant, I suppose, but I keep coming back to the water. That’s the part that gets into my head.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years and people cross them every day—my daughter reminds me of that, as if I don’t know—but when I think about driving over all that open water, the fear feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A dry canyon might make me uneasy, but it doesn’t have quite the same feeling in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if someone else was driving you over a bridge with a similar height and water underneath, but you were not driving?\n\nPatient: It’s not quite as bad if someone else is driving, I think, but I still don’t like it. I’ve actually started turning down things—gallery openings, birdwatching trips, even sometimes visits with my daughter—unless I know someone else can take me over the bridges.\n\nWhich is embarrassing, honestly, because I used to be the one who planned everything. My husband would forget half the details and I’d have the maps, the times, the parking figured out… and now I’m fussing about whether there’s a bridge on the way. So no, I wouldn’t say it feels the same as when I’m driving, but it’s still enough that I arrange my life around it more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a very high bridge over land with no water underneath?\n\nPatient: A very high bridge over land… I suppose I wouldn’t love it, no, but it’s the water that really makes my mind run away with itself.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without anything happening. My daughter says that to me too, very sensibly. But when I picture myself driving over all that open water, that’s when the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger. Over land, even if it was high, I don’t think it would hit me in quite the same way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe a little less on a bus, because I’m not the one steering, but… it’s still that moment when we get onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. That’s really what made me come in.

I can be sitting there telling myself it’s fine, everyone does this all day long, and then as soon as we’re out over the water my chest tightens and I sort of grip whatever I can. When I’m driving it’s the wheel—my hands just clamp down—and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off the bridge immediately, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.

On a bus I suppose I wouldn’t have the wheel to hold onto, so it might not be quite as sharp, but I don’t think I’d feel calm. Those long stretches over water just do something to me.

We don’t have many that I use regularly, at least not in my usual errands.\n\nI did go through some years ago on a trip—my husband used to like planning routes, and he’d always say the scenic way was better, though it usually added an hour—but I don’t remember feeling anything like what happens with those bridges. So… I’m not sure I can give you a very good answer about tunnels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would be less intense if the bridge was over land?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be less if it were over land. It’s really the open water underneath that seems to do it to me.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges have been there forever, and people drive across them all day without giving it a second thought—my daughter does, everyone does. But when I imagine myself driving over all that water, the fear just feels… much larger than the actual danger. Over land, I don’t think it would grab me the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a bridge over a very deep but dry canyon?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. A very deep canyon sounds unpleasant, I suppose, but I keep coming back to the water. That’s the part that gets into my head.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years and people cross them every day—my daughter reminds me of that, as if I don’t know—but when I think about driving over all that open water, the fear feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A dry canyon might make me uneasy, but it doesn’t have quite the same feeling in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if someone else was driving you over a bridge with a similar height and water underneath, but you were not driving?\n\nPatient: It’s not quite as bad if someone else is driving, I think, but I still don’t like it. I’ve actually started turning down things—gallery openings, birdwatching trips, even sometimes visits with my daughter—unless I know someone else can take me over the bridges.\n\nWhich is embarrassing, honestly, because I used to be the one who planned everything. My husband would forget half the details and I’d have the maps, the times, the parking figured out… and now I’m fussing about whether there’s a bridge on the way. So no, I wouldn’t say it feels the same as when I’m driving, but it’s still enough that I arrange my life around it more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a very high bridge over land with no water underneath?\n\nPatient: A very high bridge over land… I suppose I wouldn’t love it, no, but it’s the water that really makes my mind run away with itself.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without anything happening. Yes, I think it would be a bit less if I were just a passenger, because I wouldn’t have to keep my hands on the wheel and pretend I’m perfectly in control.

But what made me finally come in is really that moment when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. It’s like my chest tightens right away, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely certain I need to get off the bridge—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go. At my age I’m not strolling over bridges much—though I used to walk everywhere when I was younger, even in heels, which seems impossible now.\n\nBut what finally pushed me to come talk to you is what happens in the car. The moment my car gets onto one of those long bridges, my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp down on the wheel. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off it, right then, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less intense if you were driving on a bridge that is not over water?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less… well, I’m not completely sure, because the ones I’m avoiding are the ones over the water. It’s that stretch of open water underneath that gets to me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and everybody crosses them all day long like it’s nothing. My daughter will say, “Mother, it’s perfectly safe,” and I know that in my head. But when I’m the one facing driving over all that water, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A regular overpass or something doesn’t seem to have that same feeling for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious or fearful if you were in a tunnel?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I can’t say I’ve paid much attention to tunnels one way or the other. We don’t have many that I use regularly, at least not in my usual errands.\n\nI did go through some years ago on a trip—my husband used to like planning routes, and he’d always say the scenic way was better, though it usually added an hour—but I don’t remember feeling anything like what happens with those bridges. So… I’m not sure I can give you a very good answer about tunnels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would be less intense if the bridge was over land?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be less if it were over land. It’s really the open water underneath that seems to do it to me.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges have been there forever, and people drive across them all day without giving it a second thought—my daughter does, everyone does. But when I imagine myself driving over all that water, the fear just feels… much larger than the actual danger. Over land, I don’t think it would grab me the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a bridge over a very deep but dry canyon?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. A very deep canyon sounds unpleasant, I suppose, but I keep coming back to the water. That’s the part that gets into my head.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years and people cross them every day—my daughter reminds me of that, as if I don’t know—but when I think about driving over all that open water, the fear feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A dry canyon might make me uneasy, but it doesn’t have quite the same feeling in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if someone else was driving you over a bridge with a similar height and water underneath, but you were not driving?\n\nPatient: It’s not quite as bad if someone else is driving, I think, but I still don’t like it. I’ve actually started turning down things—gallery openings, birdwatching trips, even sometimes visits with my daughter—unless I know someone else can take me over the bridges.\n\nWhich is embarrassing, honestly, because I used to be the one who planned everything. My husband would forget half the details and I’d have the maps, the times, the parking figured out… and now I’m fussing about whether there’s a bridge on the way. So no, I wouldn’t say it feels the same as when I’m driving, but it’s still enough that I arrange my life around it more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a very high bridge over land with no water underneath?\n\nPatient: A very high bridge over land… I suppose I wouldn’t love it, no, but it’s the water that really makes my mind run away with itself.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without anything happening. My daughter says that to me too, very sensibly. But when I picture myself driving over all that open water, that’s when the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger. Over land, even if it was high, I don’t think it would hit me in quite the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were a passenger on a bus going over one of those long bridges over water?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little less on a bus, because I’m not the one steering, but… it’s still that moment when we get onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. That’s really what made me come in.\n\nI can be sitting there telling myself it’s fine, everyone does this all day long, and then as soon as we’re out over the water my chest tightens and I sort of grip whatever I can. When I’m driving it’s the wheel—my hands just clamp down—and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off the bridge immediately, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.\n\nOn a bus I suppose I wouldn’t have the wheel to hold onto, so it might not be quite as sharp, but I don’t think I’d feel calm. Those long stretches over water just do something to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were a passenger in a car going over one of those long bridges over water, compared to when you are driving yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be a bit less if I were just a passenger, because I wouldn’t have to keep my hands on the wheel and pretend I’m perfectly in control.\n\nBut what made me finally come in is really that moment when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. It’s like my chest tightens right away, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely certain I need to get off the bridge—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go. I know that sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about how my husband used to joke that I was the calm one with driving directions, and now I’m the one watching the route like a hawk… but yes, as a passenger it might be less intense, just not gone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Walking or biking… oh, goodness, I can’t quite imagine choosing to do that on one of those long bridges over water. I used to walk a lot more, you know, back when my knees were better, and my daughter keeps telling me I should do those little gentle bike things at the community center—but across a bridge like that, no.

The real thing that finally made me come in is when I’m in my car and it reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. Right at that moment my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden certainty that I have to get off—even though there’s nowhere to go. So walking or biking, I don’t know if it would be less exactly… I think being out there over the water would still set it off.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s the driving, really… though I’ve been driving a long time, you know, and my husband used to say I was the steadier one. But lately, when my car gets to one of those long bridges heading toward Miami, I just—my chest tightens right away, and my hands grip the wheel so hard. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off the bridge, even though of course there’s nowhere to get off. That’s what finally made me think I ought to come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that cause you this fear, or are there other driving situations that make you feel this way?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly those bridges, yes—especially the long ones over water. I mean, I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years, and everyone drives over them all day long without making a fuss. My neighbor’s daughter goes over into Miami constantly, like it’s nothing—she’s always rushing somewhere, though that’s young people, isn’t it?\n\nBut when I think about being out there over all that open water, it just feels… much bigger than any real danger. In my head I know it shouldn’t be such a terrible thing, but it feels enormous in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than when you're driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them the same, no. It’s really the bridges that stand out to me. I mean, I can get nervous about plenty of things—at my age there’s always some errand or appointment to think about, and parking lots have become such a circus—but that sharp, overwhelming feeling happens when I’m on those bridges, especially going toward Miami.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful in other high places, like tall buildings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, tall buildings aren’t something I deal with much anymore. Years ago I worked in an office that had quite a view, and I remember people crowding by the windows when there were fireworks, which always seemed silly to me because you could barely hear them through the glass.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed that same sort of fear just being up high in a building. It’s more—well, it’s not something I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nDoctor: Is it the act of driving on the bridges that scares you, or would you feel the same fear if you were a passenger on the same bridges?\n\nPatient: It’s worse if I’m the one driving, I think, because then I’m responsible for keeping the car steady and not hesitating. But honestly, I’ve started arranging things so someone else drives me over those bridges if I have to go.\n\nI’ve turned down gallery openings, birdwatching trips—there was one I really wanted to go to—and even some visits with my daughter unless I can get someone to take me. Which is embarrassing, because I used to be the one organizing everything, maps and times and all of it. Now I’m asking, “Are you driving?” before I even decide whether I’m going.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge, rather than driving?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really walked on one of those bridges, not the big ones going toward Miami. At my age I’m not strolling over bridges much—though I used to walk everywhere when I was younger, even in heels, which seems impossible now.\n\nBut what finally pushed me to come talk to you is what happens in the car. The moment my car gets onto one of those long bridges, my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp down on the wheel. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off it, right then, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less intense if you were driving on a bridge that is not over water?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less… well, I’m not completely sure, because the ones I’m avoiding are the ones over the water. It’s that stretch of open water underneath that gets to me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and everybody crosses them all day long like it’s nothing. My daughter will say, “Mother, it’s perfectly safe,” and I know that in my head. But when I’m the one facing driving over all that water, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A regular overpass or something doesn’t seem to have that same feeling for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious or fearful if you were in a tunnel?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I can’t say I’ve paid much attention to tunnels one way or the other. We don’t have many that I use regularly, at least not in my usual errands.\n\nI did go through some years ago on a trip—my husband used to like planning routes, and he’d always say the scenic way was better, though it usually added an hour—but I don’t remember feeling anything like what happens with those bridges. So… I’m not sure I can give you a very good answer about tunnels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would be less intense if the bridge was over land?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be less if it were over land. It’s really the open water underneath that seems to do it to me.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges have been there forever, and people drive across them all day without giving it a second thought—my daughter does, everyone does. But when I imagine myself driving over all that water, the fear just feels… much larger than the actual danger. Over land, I don’t think it would grab me the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a bridge over a very deep but dry canyon?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. A very deep canyon sounds unpleasant, I suppose, but I keep coming back to the water. That’s the part that gets into my head.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years and people cross them every day—my daughter reminds me of that, as if I don’t know—but when I think about driving over all that open water, the fear feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A dry canyon might make me uneasy, but it doesn’t have quite the same feeling in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if someone else was driving you over a bridge with a similar height and water underneath, but you were not driving?\n\nPatient: It’s not quite as bad if someone else is driving, I think, but I still don’t like it. I’ve actually started turning down things—gallery openings, birdwatching trips, even sometimes visits with my daughter—unless I know someone else can take me over the bridges.\n\nWhich is embarrassing, honestly, because I used to be the one who planned everything. My husband would forget half the details and I’d have the maps, the times, the parking figured out… and now I’m fussing about whether there’s a bridge on the way. So no, I wouldn’t say it feels the same as when I’m driving, but it’s still enough that I arrange my life around it more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a very high bridge over land with no water underneath?\n\nPatient: A very high bridge over land… I suppose I wouldn’t love it, no, but it’s the water that really makes my mind run away with itself.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without anything happening. My daughter says that to me too, very sensibly. But when I picture myself driving over all that open water, that’s when the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger. Over land, even if it was high, I don’t think it would hit me in quite the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were a passenger on a bus going over one of those long bridges over water?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little less on a bus, because I’m not the one steering, but… it’s still that moment when we get onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. That’s really what made me come in.\n\nI can be sitting there telling myself it’s fine, everyone does this all day long, and then as soon as we’re out over the water my chest tightens and I sort of grip whatever I can. When I’m driving it’s the wheel—my hands just clamp down—and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off the bridge immediately, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.\n\nOn a bus I suppose I wouldn’t have the wheel to hold onto, so it might not be quite as sharp, but I don’t think I’d feel calm. Those long stretches over water just do something to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were a passenger in a car going over one of those long bridges over water, compared to when you are driving yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be a bit less if I were just a passenger, because I wouldn’t have to keep my hands on the wheel and pretend I’m perfectly in control.\n\nBut what made me finally come in is really that moment when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. It’s like my chest tightens right away, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely certain I need to get off the bridge—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go. I know that sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about how my husband used to joke that I was the calm one with driving directions, and now I’m the one watching the route like a hawk… but yes, as a passenger it might be less intense, just not gone.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were walking or biking across one of those long bridges over water?\n\nPatient: Walking or biking… oh, goodness, I can’t quite imagine choosing to do that on one of those long bridges over water. I used to walk a lot more, you know, back when my knees were better, and my daughter keeps telling me I should do those little gentle bike things at the community center—but across a bridge like that, no.\n\nThe real thing that finally made me come in is when I’m in my car and it reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. Right at that moment my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden certainty that I have to get off—even though there’s nowhere to go. So walking or biking, I don’t know if it would be less exactly… I think being out there over the water would still set it off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It’s like my chest tightens right away, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely certain I need to get off the bridge—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go. I know that sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about how my husband used to joke that I was the calm one with driving directions, and now I’m the one watching the route like a hawk… but yes, as a passenger it might be less intense, just not gone.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were walking or biking across one of those long bridges over water?\n\nPatient: Walking or biking… oh, goodness, I can’t quite imagine choosing to do that on one of those long bridges over water. I used to walk a lot more, you know, back when my knees were better, and my daughter keeps telling me I should do those little gentle bike things at the community center—but across a bridge like that, no.\n\nThe real thing that finally made me come in is when I’m in my car and it reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. Right at that moment my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden certainty that I have to get off—even though there’s nowhere to go. So walking or biking, I don’t know if it would be less exactly… I think being out there over the water would still set it off.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould your fear of bridges be less intense if you were a passenger on a boat going over the same body of water as the bridges that scare you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 86 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response\nmanifestation: \"What finally made me come in is that the moment my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami, my chest tightens, my hands clamp the wheel, and I get this sudden certainty that I have to get off even though there’s nowhere to go.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A boat… I suppose being on the water is different, isn’t it? My husband used to like those little sightseeing boats, and I’d always worry more about whether I had a sweater than anything else.

But what really happens—the thing that made me come here—is when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. The second I’m on it, my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp onto the wheel. I get this awful, sudden feeling that I have to get off the bridge right then, even though there’s obviously nowhere to go. So a boat might be different, but it’s that bridge moment in the car that really sets it off.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s the driving, really… though I’ve been driving a long time, you know, and my husband used to say I was the steadier one. But lately, when my car gets to one of those long bridges heading toward Miami, I just—my chest tightens right away, and my hands grip the wheel so hard. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off the bridge, even though of course there’s nowhere to get off. That’s what finally made me think I ought to come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that cause you this fear, or are there other driving situations that make you feel this way?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly those bridges, yes—especially the long ones over water. I mean, I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years, and everyone drives over them all day long without making a fuss. My neighbor’s daughter goes over into Miami constantly, like it’s nothing—she’s always rushing somewhere, though that’s young people, isn’t it?\n\nBut when I think about being out there over all that open water, it just feels… much bigger than any real danger. In my head I know it shouldn’t be such a terrible thing, but it feels enormous in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than when you're driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them the same, no. It’s really the bridges that stand out to me. I mean, I can get nervous about plenty of things—at my age there’s always some errand or appointment to think about, and parking lots have become such a circus—but that sharp, overwhelming feeling happens when I’m on those bridges, especially going toward Miami.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful in other high places, like tall buildings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, tall buildings aren’t something I deal with much anymore. Years ago I worked in an office that had quite a view, and I remember people crowding by the windows when there were fireworks, which always seemed silly to me because you could barely hear them through the glass.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed that same sort of fear just being up high in a building. It’s more—well, it’s not something I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nDoctor: Is it the act of driving on the bridges that scares you, or would you feel the same fear if you were a passenger on the same bridges?\n\nPatient: It’s worse if I’m the one driving, I think, because then I’m responsible for keeping the car steady and not hesitating. But honestly, I’ve started arranging things so someone else drives me over those bridges if I have to go.\n\nI’ve turned down gallery openings, birdwatching trips—there was one I really wanted to go to—and even some visits with my daughter unless I can get someone to take me. Which is embarrassing, because I used to be the one organizing everything, maps and times and all of it. Now I’m asking, “Are you driving?” before I even decide whether I’m going.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge, rather than driving?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really walked on one of those bridges, not the big ones going toward Miami. At my age I’m not strolling over bridges much—though I used to walk everywhere when I was younger, even in heels, which seems impossible now.\n\nBut what finally pushed me to come talk to you is what happens in the car. The moment my car gets onto one of those long bridges, my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp down on the wheel. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off it, right then, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less intense if you were driving on a bridge that is not over water?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less… well, I’m not completely sure, because the ones I’m avoiding are the ones over the water. It’s that stretch of open water underneath that gets to me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and everybody crosses them all day long like it’s nothing. My daughter will say, “Mother, it’s perfectly safe,” and I know that in my head. But when I’m the one facing driving over all that water, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A regular overpass or something doesn’t seem to have that same feeling for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious or fearful if you were in a tunnel?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I can’t say I’ve paid much attention to tunnels one way or the other. We don’t have many that I use regularly, at least not in my usual errands.\n\nI did go through some years ago on a trip—my husband used to like planning routes, and he’d always say the scenic way was better, though it usually added an hour—but I don’t remember feeling anything like what happens with those bridges. So… I’m not sure I can give you a very good answer about tunnels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would be less intense if the bridge was over land?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be less if it were over land. It’s really the open water underneath that seems to do it to me.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges have been there forever, and people drive across them all day without giving it a second thought—my daughter does, everyone does. But when I imagine myself driving over all that water, the fear just feels… much larger than the actual danger. Over land, I don’t think it would grab me the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a bridge over a very deep but dry canyon?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. A very deep canyon sounds unpleasant, I suppose, but I keep coming back to the water. That’s the part that gets into my head.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years and people cross them every day—my daughter reminds me of that, as if I don’t know—but when I think about driving over all that open water, the fear feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A dry canyon might make me uneasy, but it doesn’t have quite the same feeling in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if someone else was driving you over a bridge with a similar height and water underneath, but you were not driving?\n\nPatient: It’s not quite as bad if someone else is driving, I think, but I still don’t like it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that electric would matter much. Self-driving… maybe I’d tell myself it would be easier, though I don’t know if I’d trust it, honestly. My grandson is always talking about those cars and all the computers in them, and I can barely get my phone to stop changing the brightness on me.

But really, what happens is the minute my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami, my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden certainty that I have to get off right then—even though there’s nowhere to go. So I’m not sure the kind of car would change that feeling much.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F40.228\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it’s the driving, really… though I’ve been driving a long time, you know, and my husband used to say I was the steadier one. But lately, when my car gets to one of those long bridges heading toward Miami, I just—my chest tightens right away, and my hands grip the wheel so hard. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off the bridge, even though of course there’s nowhere to get off. That’s what finally made me think I ought to come talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that cause you this fear, or are there other driving situations that make you feel this way?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly those bridges, yes—especially the long ones over water. I mean, I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years, and everyone drives over them all day long without making a fuss. My neighbor’s daughter goes over into Miami constantly, like it’s nothing—she’s always rushing somewhere, though that’s young people, isn’t it?\n\nBut when I think about being out there over all that open water, it just feels… much bigger than any real danger. In my head I know it shouldn’t be such a terrible thing, but it feels enormous in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than when you're driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them the same, no. It’s really the bridges that stand out to me. I mean, I can get nervous about plenty of things—at my age there’s always some errand or appointment to think about, and parking lots have become such a circus—but that sharp, overwhelming feeling happens when I’m on those bridges, especially going toward Miami.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful in other high places, like tall buildings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, tall buildings aren’t something I deal with much anymore. Years ago I worked in an office that had quite a view, and I remember people crowding by the windows when there were fireworks, which always seemed silly to me because you could barely hear them through the glass.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed that same sort of fear just being up high in a building. It’s more—well, it’s not something I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nDoctor: Is it the act of driving on the bridges that scares you, or would you feel the same fear if you were a passenger on the same bridges?\n\nPatient: It’s worse if I’m the one driving, I think, because then I’m responsible for keeping the car steady and not hesitating. But honestly, I’ve started arranging things so someone else drives me over those bridges if I have to go.\n\nI’ve turned down gallery openings, birdwatching trips—there was one I really wanted to go to—and even some visits with my daughter unless I can get someone to take me. Which is embarrassing, because I used to be the one organizing everything, maps and times and all of it. Now I’m asking, “Are you driving?” before I even decide whether I’m going.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if you were walking on the bridge, rather than driving?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really walked on one of those bridges, not the big ones going toward Miami. At my age I’m not strolling over bridges much—though I used to walk everywhere when I was younger, even in heels, which seems impossible now.\n\nBut what finally pushed me to come talk to you is what happens in the car. The moment my car gets onto one of those long bridges, my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp down on the wheel. I suddenly feel absolutely sure I have to get off it, right then, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be less intense if you were driving on a bridge that is not over water?\n\nPatient: I think it would be less… well, I’m not completely sure, because the ones I’m avoiding are the ones over the water. It’s that stretch of open water underneath that gets to me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and everybody crosses them all day long like it’s nothing. My daughter will say, “Mother, it’s perfectly safe,” and I know that in my head. But when I’m the one facing driving over all that water, the fear just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A regular overpass or something doesn’t seem to have that same feeling for me.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious or fearful if you were in a tunnel?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I can’t say I’ve paid much attention to tunnels one way or the other. We don’t have many that I use regularly, at least not in my usual errands.\n\nI did go through some years ago on a trip—my husband used to like planning routes, and he’d always say the scenic way was better, though it usually added an hour—but I don’t remember feeling anything like what happens with those bridges. So… I’m not sure I can give you a very good answer about tunnels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges would be less intense if the bridge was over land?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be less if it were over land. It’s really the open water underneath that seems to do it to me.\n\nI mean, I know those bridges have been there forever, and people drive across them all day without giving it a second thought—my daughter does, everyone does. But when I imagine myself driving over all that water, the fear just feels… much larger than the actual danger. Over land, I don’t think it would grab me the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a bridge over a very deep but dry canyon?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. A very deep canyon sounds unpleasant, I suppose, but I keep coming back to the water. That’s the part that gets into my head.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years and people cross them every day—my daughter reminds me of that, as if I don’t know—but when I think about driving over all that open water, the fear feels so much bigger than the actual danger. A dry canyon might make me uneasy, but it doesn’t have quite the same feeling in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if someone else was driving you over a bridge with a similar height and water underneath, but you were not driving?\n\nPatient: It’s not quite as bad if someone else is driving, I think, but I still don’t like it. I’ve actually started turning down things—gallery openings, birdwatching trips, even sometimes visits with my daughter—unless I know someone else can take me over the bridges.\n\nWhich is embarrassing, honestly, because I used to be the one who planned everything. My husband would forget half the details and I’d have the maps, the times, the parking figured out… and now I’m fussing about whether there’s a bridge on the way. So no, I wouldn’t say it feels the same as when I’m driving, but it’s still enough that I arrange my life around it more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a very high bridge over land with no water underneath?\n\nPatient: A very high bridge over land… I suppose I wouldn’t love it, no, but it’s the water that really makes my mind run away with itself.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without anything happening. My daughter says that to me too, very sensibly. But when I picture myself driving over all that open water, that’s when the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger. Over land, even if it was high, I don’t think it would hit me in quite the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were a passenger on a bus going over one of those long bridges over water?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little less on a bus, because I’m not the one steering, but… it’s still that moment when we get onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. That’s really what made me come in.\n\nI can be sitting there telling myself it’s fine, everyone does this all day long, and then as soon as we’re out over the water my chest tightens and I sort of grip whatever I can. When I’m driving it’s the wheel—my hands just clamp down—and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off the bridge immediately, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.\n\nOn a bus I suppose I wouldn’t have the wheel to hold onto, so it might not be quite as sharp, but I don’t think I’d feel calm. Those long stretches over water just do something to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were a passenger in a car going over one of those long bridges over water, compared to when you are driving yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be a bit less if I were just a passenger, because I wouldn’t have to keep my hands on the wheel and pretend I’m perfectly in control.\n\nBut what made me finally come in is really that moment when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. It’s like my chest tightens right away, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely certain I need to get off the bridge—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go. I know that sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about how my husband used to joke that I was the calm one with driving directions, and now I’m the one watching the route like a hawk… but yes, as a passenger it might be less intense, just not gone.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were walking or biking across one of those long bridges over water?\n\nPatient: Walking or biking… oh, goodness, I can’t quite imagine choosing to do that on one of those long bridges over water. I used to walk a lot more, you know, back when my knees were better, and my daughter keeps telling me I should do those little gentle bike things at the community center—but across a bridge like that, no.\n\nThe real thing that finally made me come in is when I’m in my car and it reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. Right at that moment my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden certainty that I have to get off—even though there’s nowhere to go. So walking or biking, I don’t know if it would be less exactly… I think being out there over the water would still set it off.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were a passenger on a boat going over the same body of water as the bridges that scare you?\n\nPatient: A boat… I suppose being on the water is different, isn’t it? My husband used to like those little sightseeing boats, and I’d always worry more about whether I had a sweater than anything else.\n\nBut what really happens—the thing that made me come here—is when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. The second I’m on it, my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp onto the wheel. I get this awful, sudden feeling that I have to get off the bridge right then, even though there’s obviously nowhere to go. So a boat might be different, but it’s that bridge moment in the car that really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were driving an electric or self-driving car over one of those long bridges over water?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that electric would matter much. Self-driving… maybe I’d tell myself it would be easier, though I don’t know if I’d trust it, honestly. My grandson is always talking about those cars and all the computers in them, and I can barely get my phone to stop changing the brightness on me.\n\nBut really, what happens is the minute my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami, my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden certainty that I have to get off right then—even though there’s nowhere to go. So I’m not sure the kind of car would change that feeling much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

A dry canyon might make me uneasy, but it doesn’t have quite the same feeling in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if someone else was driving you over a bridge with a similar height and water underneath, but you were not driving?\n\nPatient: It’s not quite as bad if someone else is driving, I think, but I still don’t like it. I’ve actually started turning down things—gallery openings, birdwatching trips, even sometimes visits with my daughter—unless I know someone else can take me over the bridges.\n\nWhich is embarrassing, honestly, because I used to be the one who planned everything. My husband would forget half the details and I’d have the maps, the times, the parking figured out… and now I’m fussing about whether there’s a bridge on the way. So no, I wouldn’t say it feels the same as when I’m driving, but it’s still enough that I arrange my life around it more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel afraid if you were on a very high bridge over land with no water underneath?\n\nPatient: A very high bridge over land… I suppose I wouldn’t love it, no, but it’s the water that really makes my mind run away with itself.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without anything happening. My daughter says that to me too, very sensibly. But when I picture myself driving over all that open water, that’s when the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger. Over land, even if it was high, I don’t think it would hit me in quite the same way.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were a passenger on a bus going over one of those long bridges over water?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little less on a bus, because I’m not the one steering, but… it’s still that moment when we get onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. That’s really what made me come in.\n\nI can be sitting there telling myself it’s fine, everyone does this all day long, and then as soon as we’re out over the water my chest tightens and I sort of grip whatever I can. When I’m driving it’s the wheel—my hands just clamp down—and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off the bridge immediately, even though of course there’s nowhere to go.\n\nOn a bus I suppose I wouldn’t have the wheel to hold onto, so it might not be quite as sharp, but I don’t think I’d feel calm. Those long stretches over water just do something to me.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were a passenger in a car going over one of those long bridges over water, compared to when you are driving yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be a bit less if I were just a passenger, because I wouldn’t have to keep my hands on the wheel and pretend I’m perfectly in control.\n\nBut what made me finally come in is really that moment when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. It’s like my chest tightens right away, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely certain I need to get off the bridge—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go. I know that sounds dramatic, and then I start thinking about how my husband used to joke that I was the calm one with driving directions, and now I’m the one watching the route like a hawk… but yes, as a passenger it might be less intense, just not gone.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were walking or biking across one of those long bridges over water?\n\nPatient: Walking or biking… oh, goodness, I can’t quite imagine choosing to do that on one of those long bridges over water. I used to walk a lot more, you know, back when my knees were better, and my daughter keeps telling me I should do those little gentle bike things at the community center—but across a bridge like that, no.\n\nThe real thing that finally made me come in is when I’m in my car and it reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. Right at that moment my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden certainty that I have to get off—even though there’s nowhere to go. So walking or biking, I don’t know if it would be less exactly… I think being out there over the water would still set it off.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were a passenger on a boat going over the same body of water as the bridges that scare you?\n\nPatient: A boat… I suppose being on the water is different, isn’t it? My husband used to like those little sightseeing boats, and I’d always worry more about whether I had a sweater than anything else.\n\nBut what really happens—the thing that made me come here—is when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. The second I’m on it, my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp onto the wheel. I get this awful, sudden feeling that I have to get off the bridge right then, even though there’s obviously nowhere to go. So a boat might be different, but it’s that bridge moment in the car that really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be less intense if you were driving an electric or self-driving car over one of those long bridges over water?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that electric would matter much. Self-driving… maybe I’d tell myself it would be easier, though I don’t know if I’d trust it, honestly. My grandson is always talking about those cars and all the computers in them, and I can barely get my phone to stop changing the brightness on me.\n\nBut really, what happens is the minute my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami, my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden certainty that I have to get off right then—even though there’s nowhere to go. So I’m not sure the kind of car would change that feeling much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A cliff… well, I haven’t been standing at the edge of many cliffs lately, unless you count that overlook my sister made me stop at years ago on a trip out west—she loved taking pictures, and I was always telling her to step back.

But it’s not quite the same in my mind. I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day. I can say that sitting here. But when I think about driving over all that open water, the fear just feels much bigger than the actual danger. A high place not over water might make me cautious, but it’s that open water under the bridge that really gets built up in my head.

My daughter says that to me too, very sensibly. But when I picture myself driving over all that open water, that’s when the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger. Maybe a little, if it wasn’t toward Miami, just because I wouldn’t have all those memories of that route, you know? My niece lives down that way and the traffic is always something—everyone rushing, lanes changing, and I start thinking about whether I left early enough, whether I should’ve used the restroom before leaving, all that nonsense.
